class Solution:
    def construct(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> 'Node':
        level = len(grid)

        if level >= 1:
            root = self.buildTree(grid1, 0, level, 0, level, level)
        return root

        tl = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        tr = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)
        bl = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def construct(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> 'Node':
        level = len(grid)
        if level == 0:
            return None
        return self.buildTree(grid, 0, level, 0, level, level)

    def buildTree(self, grid, rs, re, cs, ce, level):
        if level == 1:
            return Node(grid[rs][cs], True, None, None, None, None)

        next_level = level // 2
        tl = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        tr = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)
        bl = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        br = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)

        if tl.isLeaf and tr.isLeaf and bl.isLeaf and br.isLeaf and tl.val == tr.val == bl.val == br.val:
            return Node(tl.val, True, None, None, None, None)
        return Node(tl.val, False, tl, tr, bl, br)
```

Let's look at LeetCode 427 official solution in Python? I remember:
```
class Solution:
    def construct(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> 'Node':
        def dfs(n, r, c):
            if n == 1:
                return Node(grid[r][c] == 1, True, None, None, None, None)
            topLeft = dfs(n // 2, r, c)
            topRight = dfs(n // 2, r, c + n // 2)
            bottomLeft = dfs(n // 2, r + n // 2, c)
            bottomRight = dfs(n // 2, r + n // 2, c + n // 2)
            if topLeft.isLeaf and topRight.isLeaf and bottomLeft.isLeaf and bottomRight.isLeaf and topLeft.val == topRight.val == bottomLeft.val == bottomRight.val:
                return Node(topLeft.val, True, None, None, None, None)
            return Node(False, False, topLeft, topRight, bottomLeft, bottomRight)
        return dfs(len(grid), 0, 0)
```
They set internal val = False. But problem says any value. So okay.

If `tl.val` is 1, `tr.val` is 1, `bl.val` is 1, `br.val` is 1 -> True. Good.
If `tl.val` is 0, `tr.val` is 0, `bl.val` is 1, `br.val` is 0 -> False. Good.

Potential issue: The code's `buildTree` uses `level` parameter but also uses `re - next_level` and `ce - next_level` to compute mid. If `level` is not equal to `re - rs` or `ce - cs`, the recursion would be wrong. But initial call and recursive calls maintain invariant. Let's prove:
- Initial: rs=0, re=level, cs=0, ce=level, so re-rs=level, ce-cs=level.
- Recursive calls:
  - tl: rs same, re' = re - next_level. re' - rs = (re - rs) - next_level = level - next_level = next_level (since level=2*next_level). Good.
  - tr: rs same, re' = re - next_level, re'-rs=next_level. cs' = ce - next_level, ce' = ce, ce'-cs'=next_level.
  - bl: rs' = re - next_level, re'=re, re'-rs'=next_level. cs same, ce'=ce-next_level, ce'-cs=next_level.
  - br: rs'=re-next_level, re'=re, cs'=ce-next_level, ce'=ce, both next_level.
So invariant holds.

The final answer should be code only. We can include `from typing import List` and class. Should we convert grid value to bool? Let's decide. The original code's `Node(grid[rs][cs], True, ...)` passes int. In LeetCode, Node.val is boolean. To be safe and semantically correct, use `grid[rs][cs] == 1` or `bool(grid[rs][cs])`. Since grid values are 0/1, `bool(grid[rs][cs])` works. But if we change to bool, the equality check `tl.val == tr.val == bl.val == br.val` still works. The internal node val = tl.val bool. Good.

```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def construct(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> 'Node':
        level = len(grid)
        if level == 0:
            return None
        return self.buildTree(grid, 0, level, 0, level, level)

    def buildTree(self, grid, rs, re, cs, ce, level):
        if level == 1:
            return Node(grid[rs][cs] == 1, True, None, None, None, None)

        next_level = level // 2
        tl = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        tr = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)
        bl = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        br = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)

        if tl.isLeaf and tr.isLeaf and bl.isLeaf and br.isLeaf and tl.val == tr.val == bl.val == br.val:
            return Node(tl.val, True, None, None, None, None)
        return Node(tl.val, False, tl, tr, bl, br)
```

Need maybe if `level == 0` return None. But if LeetCode constraints n>=1, okay. If we include `if level == 0`, no harm. But if grid empty, `level=0`, return None. If grid = [[]], level=1 but grid[0][0] error. Not in constraints.

Potential issue: `Node(grid[rs][cs] == 1, True, None, None, None, None)` returns bool. Good.

Potential issue: `if tl.isLeaf and tr.isLeaf and bl.isLeaf and br.isLeaf and tl.val == tr.val == bl.val == br.val:` If tl.val is bool, chained comparison works. Good.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def construct(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> 'Node':
        level = len(grid)
        if level == 0:
            return None
        return self.buildTree(grid, 0, level, 0, level, level)

    def buildTree(self, grid, rs, re, cs, ce, level):
        if level == 1:
            return Node(grid[rs][cs] == 1, True, None, None, None, None)

        next_level = level // 2

        tl = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        tr = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)
        bl = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        br = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def construct(self, grid: List[List[int]]) -> 'Node':
        level = len(grid)
        if level == 0:
            return None
        return self.buildTree(grid, 0, level, 0, level, level)

    def buildTree(self, grid, rs, re, cs, ce, level):
        if level == 1:
            return Node(grid[rs][cs] == 1, True, None, None, None, None)

        next_level = level // 2
        tl = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        tr = self.buildTree(grid, rs, re - next_level, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)
        bl = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, cs, ce - next_level, next_level)
        br = self.buildTree(grid, re - next_level, re, ce - next_level, ce, next_level)

        if tl.isLeaf and tr.isLeaf and bl.isLeaf and br.isLeaf and tl.val == tr.val == bl.val == br.val:
            return Node(tl.val, True, None, None, None, None)
        return Node(tl.val, False, tl, tr, bl, br)
